THIRD ANNUAL REPORT 

1959 

OFFICE OF 
ADMINISTRATIVE PROCEDURE 



A Unit of 

THE OFFICE OF LEGAL COUNSEL 

DEPARTMENT OF JUSTICE 

Washington, D.C. 



OFFICE OF THE ATTORNEY GENERAL 

[Order 142-57] 
Establishment of Office of Administrative Procedure 

Establishment of Office of Administrative Procedure. 1. There is hereby 
established in the Department of Justice an Office of Administrative 
Procedure which shall be a component of and under the administrative 
supervision of the Office of Legal Counsel. 

Director. 2. The Office shall be headed by a Director v^ho will act 
in an advisory capacity in carrying out the purposes of the Office. 

Purposes of the Office, 3. With a view to achieving improvements in 
administrative procedures within the Executive departments and 
agencies of the Federal Government, the Office shall: 

(a) Garry on continuous studies of the adequacy of the procedures 
by which Federal departments and agencies determine the rights, 
duties, and privileges of persons; 

(b) Initiate cooperative effort among the departments and agencies 
and their respective bars to develop and adopt so far as practicable 
uniform rules of practice and procedure; 

(c) Gollect and publish facts and statistics concerning the procedures 
of the departments and agencies; 

(d) Assist departments and agencies in the formulation and improve- 
ment of their administrative procedures. 

Cooperation by Departments and Agencies, the Bar^ and other interested persons. 
4. The Director, in consultation with the Attorney General, is author- 
ized to make appropriate arrangements for securing the cooperation 
and advice of representatives of the departments and agencies, the bar, 
and other interested persons in connection with the performance of his 
duties. 

Reports. 5. The Director shall render an annual report for circula- 
tion to the departments and agencies, and such other reports as the 
Attorney General may from time to time request. 

Dated: February 6, 1957. 



Herbert Brownell, Jr., 
Attorney General 

[22 F.R. 998]

IV 



FOREWORD 

The Third Annual Report of the Office of Administrative Procedure 
covers the calendar year 1959. 

Part I explains the nature and purpose of the Office by reviewing 
briefly the recommendations which led to its estabHshment in 1957 and 
describing generally its present functions and organization. 

Part II contains a summary of the major activities of the Office 
during 1959. The number of projects reported in this summary does 
not include what actually is the Office's major day-to-day activity, 
that is, its function as a central source of information and general ref- 
erence service on agency procedures for all agencies and other persons 
interested in the administrative process. The number of informal 
requests for information and advice with respect to problems in admin- 
istrative procedures has increased steadily since the Office was estab- 
lished. These informal contacts are reflected in the summary figures 
only in those cases in which such requests developed into full-scale 
projects. 

Part III of this report contains the Office's third annual compilation 
of statistics on administrative proceedings. The 133 pages of tables, 
together with the explanatory text which precedes them, represent a 
continuation and expansion of the volume, backlog, and time-study 
data first presented as Part II of the 1957 Annual Report. This infor- 
mation is compiled on a fiscal year basis, since that is the basis upon 
which such figures ordinarily are maintained by the agencies and are 
most useful to them. 

In issuing this report, the Office gratefully acknowledges the con- 
tinuing support, cooperation, and invaluable guidance of the Bureau 
of the Budget and that of the agencies, their liaison officers, and the 
many other persons who have worked with the Office toward solution 
of the problems presented. The list of agency liaison officers follows.

THIRD ANNUAL REPORT 

of the 

OFFICE OF ADMINISTRATIVE PROCEDURE 

1959 

PART I— BACKGROUND, FUNCTION, AND 
ORGANIZATION OF THE OFFICE 

For many years there has been increasing public interest in improv- 
ing the procedures by which Federal executive departments and ad- 
ministrative agencies determine the rights-,' privileges, and obligations 
of private persons, that is, procedures for agency rulemaking and 
adjudication. The administrative process, so-called, now has grown to 
such proportions that problems of inadequacy of procedures and inor- 
dinate expense and delay in administrative proceedings, wherever such 
problems may exist, are matters of critical concern. 

During the fiscal year 1959 Federal agencies commenced 37,393 new 
proceedings of kinds in which hearing examiners appointed pursuant 
to section 1 1 of the Administrative Procedure Act conduct hearings 
and render decisions or initial or recommended decisions. This figure 
excludes the many thousands of hearing proceedings before boards and 
ofl?icers other than APA examiners ^ and countless informal procedures 
whereby rights, privileges, and obligations are determined without 
hearings.^ 

The problems of the rapidly expanding administrative process have 
been the subject of a number of broad inquiries over the past 25 years. 
These studies and the passage of the Administrative Procedure Act in 
1946^ have contributed greatly to the standardization and improve- 
ment of agency rulemaking and adjudication. The Federal Register 
attests almost daily to the constant process of agency self-improvement 
through revision of agency procedures and rules. However, the num- 
ber and complexity of problems in this field have increased with the 
volume of cases, perhaps reflecting underlying defects, and there are 



' For example, the approximately 14,000 Immigration and Naturalization Service 
exclusion and expulsion proceedings before Special Inquiry Officers designated by 
the Attorney General pursuant to 8 U.S.G. § 1101(b)(4) (1958). 

-During fiscal 1959, the FCC, for example, granted without hearing 430 broadcast 
TV, FM, and AM new station applications, including non-commercial, educational 
and TV translator applications. 

3 5 U.S.G. §§ 1001-1011 (1958). 

1 



many indications that considerable pressures are building behind the 
universal desire for and recognition of the need for specific improve- 
ment. Far-reaching change and development in the administrative 
process are likely over the next few years. 

Each of the groups which have undertaken comprehensive studies of 
the administrative process, beginning with the Attorney General's 
Committee on Administrative Procedure in 1939-1941, has recom- 
mended creation of an Office of Administrative Procedure in the 
Executive branch to serve as a central source of information concern- 
ing agency procedures and to coordinate the cooperative efforts of the 
agencies toward solution of their common problems. The more signifi- 
cant of such recommendations prior to establishment of the present 
Office may be listed as follows: 

1. Report of the Attorney General's Committee on Administrative Procedure 
(1941) ^ To establish an independent Office of Federal Administra- 
tive Procedure directed by a board of (1) a judge of the Court of Ap- 
peals for the District of Columbia Circuit, (2) the Director of the Ad- 
ministrative Office of the United States Courts, and (3) a Director of 
Federal Administrative Procedure, to devote "continuing and system- 
atic attention" to the agencies' "common procedural problems," and to 
exercise important functions with respect to selection and removal of 
hearing commissioners. 

2. Report of the Task Force on Regulatory Commissions of the Commission on 
Organization of the Executive Branch of the Government (1949). — To establish 
an Office of Administrative Procedure in the Executive Office of the 
President, - to be headed by a Director aided by an advisory committee 
including the Director of the Bureau of the Budget and the Director 
of the Administrative Office of the United States Courts, to study each 
of the regulatory agencies (as distinguished from the Executive depart- 
ments) on a regular basis for the purpose of "reviewing its organiza- 
tion, operations, workload and delays, methods and procedures, 
supervision of staff, and similar matters" and "comparing the methods 
and performance of similar agencies." 

3. Report of the Commission on Organization of the Executive Branch of the 
Government on the Independent Regulatory Commissions (1949) ^. — That "the 
Administrative Management Division of the Bureau of the Budget 
should, with the aid of carefully selected legal consultants, suggest 
ways and means to improve and thereby reduce the cost of * * * ad- 
ministrative disposition of controversies before all Government agencies." 

4. Report of the Committee to Consider the Desirability, of an Office of Admin- 
istrative Procedure of the President's Conference on Administrative Procedure 
(1953): — To establish an Office of Administrative Procedure in the 
Justice Department to "carry on continuous studies looking to the im- 



'^S. Doc. No. 8, 77th Cong., 1st Sess. (1941), p. 123. 
^H. Doc. No. 116, 81st Cong., 1st Sess. 



provement of Federal administrative procedures, and to act as a cen- 
tral source of information" on administrative procedures, with specific 
direction to compile statistics, formulate and recommend uniform rules, 
and initiate a cooperative effort v^ith the Civil Service Commission and 
the agencies for improvement in the hearing examiner function. 

5. First Report of the President's Conference on Administrative Procedure 
(1953). — To establish an Office of Administrative Procedure in the 

Justice Department, consisting of a Director and staff, to carry on con- 
tinuous studies of the adequacy of procedures, to develop uniform 
rules, to collect statistics, and to assist agencies in improving their 
rules. 

6. Report of the Task Force on Legal Services and Procedure of the Commission 
on Organization of the Executive Branch of the Government (1954)^. — To estab- 
lish an Office of Legal Services and Procedure in the Justice Depart- 
ment with statutory authority to collect information through access to 
agency files and to compel agency comphance with its instructions for 
simplifying, clarifying, and making uniform agency rules of "substance 
and procedure" and for agency compliance with the public information 
requirements of the Administrative Code, to receive and investigate 
complaints, to perform certain functions with respect to recruitment 
and performance of Government attorneys other than hearing exam- 
iners, and to recommend consolidation or reorganization of agency 
legal staffs wherever appropriate. 

7. Report of the Commission on Organization of the Executive Branch of the 
Government on Legal Services and Procedure (1955) \ — Adopting the 1954 
Task Force recommendation without substantial change. 

8. Report of the President's Conference on Administrative Procedure (1955)^, — 
Repeating the recommendation and comment set forth in its First Re- 
port in 1953. 

Acting upon the recommendations of the President's Conference and 
the Hoover Commission, Attorney General Brownell, in February 1957, 
established on a limited and experimental basis the Office of Admin- 
istrative Procedure in the Department of Justice. The order establish- 
ing the Office is reprinted herein on the back of the title page. 

The original staff of the Office consisted of a Director, two attor- 
neys, and two secretaries, operating as a component of and under the 
administrative supervision of the Office of Legal Counsel. From Sep- 
tember 1959 through the end of the year, the Director position has 
been vacant, and the Office has consisted of two attorneys and one 
secretary. 

The purpose of the Office is to assist the Executive departments and 
agencies, through continuing studies, collection and publication of facts 



^Recommendation No. 61, Report of the Task Force, p. 232. 

^ Recommendation No. 49, Report of the Commission, pp. 82-84. 

« Report, Recommendation No. A. 1, pp. 3, 4, 46-48. 



and statistics, and consulting and advisory services, in improving the 
procedures whereby they determine private rights, privileges, and obli- 
gations through rulemaking and adjudication. More specifically, the 
Office is charged with the functions of studying continuously the ade- 
quacy of agency procedures, of developing simple, economical, and 
standard procedures, and of assisting the agencies in the formulation 
and improvement of specific procedures, all to the end that the public 
business may be conducted fairly, with economy, efficiency, and 
dispatch. 

To assist in carrying out these responsibilities, each of 50 depart- 
ments and agencies has cooperated with the Office by designating a 
responsible staff officer to maintain official liaison. The list of liaison 
officers appears herein at page vi. This arrangement provides informal 
contact with the agencies and furnishes the Office current information 
relating to the major activities of the agencies in the field of admin- 
istrative procedures. Also, the Office is in frequent contact with bar 
associations, administrative law practitioners, law schools, specialized 
agency bar groups, and other organizations and individuals interested 
in improvement in the administrative process. 

The purview of the Office is the whole field of administrative pro- 
cedures in all of the executive departments and administrative agen- 
cies of the Federal Government. In its broad sense, the term 
"administrative procedures" encompasses a wide range of governmental 
activity. The operation of the Office of Administrative Procedure on 
its present limited and experimental basis imposes obvious limits upon 
the activities of the Office at both ends of this range. 

Routine, "purely administrative" procedures such as procedures for 
the efficient handling and recording of visa applications are "admin- 
istrative procedures" in the broad sense. However, in general, prob- 
lems in connection therewith are perhaps more appropriate for the 
attention of expert administrators and management consultants than 
for the lawyers of the Office of Administrative Procedure. 

Similarly, fundamental questions as to the most effective form and 
distribution of regulatory powers for the accomplishment of particular 
governmental functions, including questions of the need for creation or 
abolition of administrative agencies, are questions of "administrative 
procedures." Obviously, these questions are beyond the scope of the 
studies of the Office in its present form, except to the extent that in- 
formation developed by the Office may be incidentally helpful. 

The Office's mandate with respect to improving the procedures by 
which "Federal departments and agencies determine the rights, duties, 
and privileges of persons" concerns primarily procedures for agency 
rulemaking and adjudication, formal and informal, and matters inci- 
dent thereto including notice and publication and procedures for 
judicial review of agency action. 

^ 4 



The authority of the Office is to request information pertinent and 
useful to its proper functions, to consult with agencies and other per- 
sons interested in administrative procedures, to organize cooperative, 
efforts among such persons to improve administrative procedures, and 
to report, advise, and recommend. The Office has no power to de- 
mand, to impose, or to compel. 



PART II— OPERATIONS DURING THE 
CALENDAR YEAR 1959 

In 1959, the Office completed 131 assignments and projects of all 
kinds. Of these, 52 involved legislative proposals, 19 concerned revi- 
sion of agency procedural rules, 1 7 consisted of study and preparation 
of reports and recommendations on particular problems in administra- 
tive procedures, 2 involved appellate litigation, and 41 covered a wide 
range of miscellaneous special assignments. In addition, informal re- 
quests for information, advice, and assistance in the interpretation and 
application of procedural statutes and rules and requests for help in 
connection with procedural problems were received by the Office with 
increasing frequency and were handled on a day-to-day basis, generally 
through direct and informal communications. 

Legislation 

Because the extent to which an agency can revise and improve its 
procedures depends in large measure upon the limitations of procedural 
provisions imposed by the statutes administered by the agency, pro- 
posed legislation is of primary importance to the functions of the 
Office. During the year the Office did work in connection with 52 
bills pending before the 86th Congress or draft bills prepared for sub- 
sequent introduction concerning agency procedures and judicial review 
thereof. This work consisted of the preparation of analyses, comments, 
and suggestions, the drafting of proposed language and, in many cases, 
conferences with representatives of the agencies involved and the staff of 
Congressional committees. 

In this activity, no comment is made by the Office upon the merits 
of any proposed regulatory scheme. The interest of the Office is con- 
fined to whether the procedural and judicial review provisions of the 
proposed statute accord with accepted concepts of due process, whether 
they will promote fairness, efficiency, and economy, whether they in- 
volve unnecessary dissimilarity to other procedures for the performance 
of similar functons, and whether the language is precise and free of 
ambiguity. 

Rules Studies 

The principal projects of the Office during the year in connection 
with improvement of rules of practice involved cooperation with three 
Executive departments and three independent agencies in completely 
overhauling rules of practice for several different kinds of proceedings 
and assistance in drafting rules to govern new proceedings under the 



Labor-Management Reporting and Disclosure Act of 1959. Other 
rules studies concerned specific problems referred to the Office by a 
number of agencies relative to particular features of their procedural 
rules. In addition, much of the activity of the Office in response to 
informal, day-to day requests for information and assistance concerned 
agency rules of practice. 

Special Studies 

Included in the 17 projects referred to above as matters in which 
the Office prepared special reports and recommendations were studies 
of certain public information practices of Executive agencies, several 
assignments with respect to the development of standards of conduct 
for administrative proceedings, activity in connection with procedures 
under the Renegotiation Act of 1951, study of the role of public coun- 
sel in particular administrative proceedings, and a number of confer- 
ences with representatives of the Bureau of the Budget and other 
agencies concerning various delegations and transfers of authority. 

Appellate Litigation 

Two of the assignments of the Office during the year involved the 
preparation of analyses and conferences with the Attorney General, the 
Solicitor General, and the agencies involved concerning litigation de- 
termined in 1959 which had significant impact upon important con- 
cepts of administrative law. 

Miscellaneous Assignments 

The 41 miscellaneous undertakings of the Office during the year 
covered a wide range of subjects from participation in the activities of 
the Subcommittee on Administrative Practice and Procedure of the 
Senate Judiciary Committee, the House Legislative Oversight Subcom- 
mittee, the Judicial Conference Interim Committee on Establishment 
of a President's Conference on Administrative Procedure, the Amer- 
ican Bar Association, and the Federal Bar Association, to such inci- 
dental assignments as assistance in the preparation of a number of 
speeches and papers dealing with governmental processes. 

As the Office concluded its third year of operation, it was evident 
that its activities were not settling into any fixed pattern, but rather 
were becoming more various as public interest in and agency efforts 
toward improvements in administrative procedures increased and 
multiplied. 



PART III— STATISTICS ON ADMINISTRATIVE 
PROCEEDINGS 

It is self-evident that no systematic approach to improvement in the 
procedures of administrative agencies can be undertaken without a 
comprehensive understanding of the business of the agencies. Because 
of the magnitude of the administrative process, much of the knowledge 
indispensable to such understanding can be presented intelligibly only 
in statistical form. In this vast area there are many problems which 
require a kind of guidance which only statistical information can 
provide. 

Statistics generally involve inherent limitations which can lead to 
uninformative results and sometimes to misleading results. The basic 
difficulty, of course, lies in the fact that the things being counted sel- 
dom are identical, and their differences are lost when they are sub- 
merged in a figure. This may be particularly true of judicial statistics 
and perhaps even more true of statistics on proceedings conducted by 
administrative agencies, because of the greater variety of the proceed- 
ings of administrative agencies. 

However, for the past 37 years judicial statistics have proven invalu- 
able to the Judicial Conference in improving the business of the courts. 
In the brief experience of the Office of Administrative Procedure its 
statistics on administrative proceedings have received widespread ac- 
ceptance and have been an indispensable aid in studying agency 
processes. They are not an end in themselves and are rarely even a 
direct means to specific solutions. However, used intelligently as a 
tool to complement traditional specific study, they do provide emphasis 
and guidance in locating problems, and sometimes point the way 
toward solutions. 

The proposals referred to in Part I hereof to create an Office of Ad- 
ministrative Procedure recommended that the office serve as a central 
source of information concerning administrative procedures and gener- 
ally contemplated that such office would collect and publish facts and 
statistics relative to administrative proceedings. Paragraph 3(c) of 
Administrative Order 142-57 establishing the Office in the Department 
of Justice directs the Office to perform such function. 

In compliance with that directive, the Office, in 1957, instituted a 
continuing system of statistics relative to formal proceedings before 
hearing examiners appointed pursuant to section 11 of the Administra- 
tive Procedure Act in the 21 departments and agencies which regu- 
larly employ such examiners. 

Each agency was asked to assort the proceedings before its exam- 

578182 O-60— 2 ^ 



iners during the year into kinds or types according to similarities in 
scope, function, complexity, relief sought, etc., as well as by statutory 
authority. Requested data were furnished separately for each kind of 
proceeding. The agencies' division into kinds of proceedings is the 
division employed in the Office's statistical tables, although character- 
izations have been changed in some instances for purposes of abbrevia- 
tion. 

This compilation, like the 1957 and 1958 compilations, is limited to 
kinds of administrative proceedings in which: 

(1) opportunity is afforded for hearing before an APA section 11 

examiner, and 

(2) such examiner, in normal course, issues a decision or initial 
or recommended decision. 

Proceedings wherein evidentiary hearings are conducted by the 
agency itself or by members of its staff other than APA examiners 
are not included. 

The original compilation was limited further as to the stages of pro- 
ceedings for which data were reported. It encompassed only so much 
of the above described proceedings as was in the hands of hearing 
examiners, excluding whatever stages preceded assignment of a pro- 
ceeding to an examiner as well as agency consideration of the case 
following the examiner's decision. This limitation was abandoned in 
the 1958 and 1959 compilations. Both the 1958 and 1959 compila- 
tions cover all stages of proceedings, from commencement to final 
agency disposition. 

As used herein, the term "proceeding" means a matter which in 
normal course is referred to an examiner for evidentiary hearing, by 
oral or written presentation, and for recommended or initial decision 
by him, even though it may be a consolidated proceeding involving 
more than one petition, application, or complaint. Therefore, although 
a matter may involve several claimants, applicants, or respondents and 
several docket numbers, if it looks toward resolution by means of a 
joint hearing and a single decision binding upon all parties, it is re- 
ported as one ''proceeding." 

The question of when a matter becomes a "proceeding," that is, 
when it is commenced, for purposes of this report is important in con- 
nection with Parts A and B of the tables. Under some administra- 
tive procedures, a proceeding as herein defined is commenced upon 
the filing of a complaint, petition, appHcation, etc., as in the case of 
court actions. These are cases in which opportunity for hearing ordi- 
narily is afforded on every petition or complaint filed. For purposes 
of this report these cases are counted as proceedings from the date 
of initial filing, and the age of such proceedings is computed from 
that date. 

Other procedures do not necessarily contemplate hearing in every 

10 



case. For example, depending upon statutory authority, petitions or 
applications are often subject to summary administrative grant or 
denial without hearing, and are designated as proceedings in which 
there will be a hearing only where issues are presented which pre- 
clude summary administrative determination. These matters are not 
counted as proceedings for the purposes of this report until they reach 
the point where a determination is made that they are to be resolved 
by the hearing process. In these cases, the date of that determination, 
whether it be by designation for hearing, entry upon the hearing docket, 
or referral to the office of hearing examiners, is considered the date 
upon which the proceeding was commenced, and its age is computed 
therefrom. 

Part A — Agency Volume and Backlog Data 

Table I provides a general view of the entire "APA formal case" 
process in terms of each agency's volume and backlog of proceedings, 
by kinds of proceedings. The first column indicates the number of 
pending cases with which each agency began the fiscal years 1958 and 
1959, whatever their stages of completion at that time. The second 
and third columns report each year's input in new cases commenced 
and cases which had been closed, during the year or prior thereto, but 
which were reopened during the fiscal year. The fourth column shows 
output, that is the number of cases closed during the year, whatever 
may have been the manner of their disposition, and the fifth column 
reports the balance remaining on the docket at the end of each year. 

The Civil Aeronautics Board, the Social Security Administration, and 
the Office of Alien Property substantially reduced their backlog of cases 
in 1959. The figures show sizable increases in the number of cases 
pending before the Federal Power Commission, the Interstate Com- 
merce Commission, the National Labor Relations Board, and the 
Securities and Exchange Commission. 

The most critical situation reflected in this table is that of the Fed- 
eral Power Commission. Of a total of 1,867 proceedings pending during 
the 2-year period from July 1, 1957 to June 30, 1959, 204 were con- 
cluded during that time, leaving a balance at the end of the year 1959 
of 1,663 pending cases, almost four times the Commission's backlog on 
July 1, 1957. 

Table 2 demonstrates the age of proceedings pending at the end of each 
fiscal year. The age of each case was measured from its commencement, 
as that term is explained above. The demonstration is by age grouping 
because it is felt that, for purposes of these tables, this technique is prob- 
ably more indicative than average or median figures. 

The grand total figures show that less than one half of all proceed- 
ings pending on June 30 of each year had been pending before the 

11 



agencies for more than 6 months. Less than 5% had been pending 
more than 3 years. 

Table 3 shows the manner in which the agencies disposed of the 
63,249 proceedings which were terminated during the 2-year period. 
Less than 9% of all cases concluded traveled the full route through 
hearing, examiner decision, and agency final decision on the merits. 
More than 30% were terminated by default, withdrawal, settlement, or 
other form of consent or agreement, actual or presumed. A total of 
36,257 proceedings were disposed of by examiner decisions which be- 
came final without agency review. 26,033 of these were Social Security 
cases, and 2,949 were Bureau of Indian Affairs cases disposed of by 
Examiners of Inheritance. 

Part B — Time Intervals in Proceedings Terminated During the Tear by Agency 
Final Decision on the Merits After Hearing and Examiner Decision 

The four tables included in Part B provide time-study data on only 
those proceedings which ''traveled the full route" through hearing, 
examiner decision, and agency final decision on the merits, and were 
thereby closed during the fiscal years 1958 and 1959, that is, the 
5,403 proceedings reported in the second column of Table 3. 

Table 4 illustrates the over-all time involved in the handling of each 
such case, from the date the proceeding was commenced to the date 
of the agency's final decision which closed the case, 3.6% were in proc- 
ess more than three years. 9.5% required more than two years. More 
than one-third were concluded in from one to two years, and 38% in 
6 to 12 months. 

The time intervals indicated in Table 4 do not include time in- 
volved in the disposition of petitions for reopening, rehearing, or re- 
consideration. While it is recognized that the volume of such peti- 
tions and the time and eff'ort involved in consideration thereof may be 
substantial, and that they may impose considerable burdens upon 
some agencies, attempts to measure the time involved in post-deci- 
sional processes should be subordinated to the measurement of the 
time it takes to get a final decision in the first instance, and perhaps 
should follow a survey of the extent of such processes. These prob- 
lems are left for a more detailed compilation in a subsequent year. 

If a proceeding, in fact, is reopened, it is added to input just as new 
cases are, for purposes of these statistics. 

Table 5 shows how long it took, after a proceeding was commenced, 
to complete the evidentiary hearing. For purposes of this table, the 
hearing is considered completed on the last day on which testimony 
or documentary or other evidence was put into the record, whether 
or not oral argument on the record was to follow. 

This is a new table, not included in the previous compilations of 

12 



the Office. It replaces last year's Table 5, which demonstrated the 
time interval from commencement of a proceeding to its assignment 
to a hearing examiner. 

The 1958 report shows that 21% of the proceedings reported in that 
table were assigned to examiners simultaneously with their commence- 
ment (all of the proceedings of the Federal Trade Commission, Social 
Security Administration, Bureau of Indian Affairs, Office of AUen 
Property, and two of the twelve kinds of proceedings of the Civil 
Aeronautics Board). Another 22% of the proceedings reported were 
assigned to examiners within a month after commencement, most of 
these within 3 or 4 days. The bulk of the remaining 57% of the pro- 
ceedings reported in last year's Table 5 were assigned to examiners 
within 3 months after commencement. 

In dividing a proceeding into its several stages or phases, from an 
agency management point of view one might reasonably consider the 
period from original filing to assignment to an examiner for hearing 
and decision, that is, the period of "administrative processing," as the 
first basic division. The hearing examiner stage might logically be 
the second, that is, the period from the time the agency temporarily 
relinquishes control of the case to an examiner until the examiner dis- 
poses of the matter insofar as he is concerned by issuing his decision, 
thereby returning the matter to the agency for review. The third 
phase then would consist of the period of agency consideration looking 
toward a final decision. Table 5 in the 1958 compilation measured 
the time involved in the first of these three phases. 

Another basic division from a party's point of view might be, first, 
how much time is involved, after filing, before the matter is heard, 
and second, how long after that does it take to get a decision. Table 

5 of this compilation measures the first of these two time intervals. 
Table 6 also is a new table, included for the first time in the 1959 

compilation. It demonstrates the period of time from the examiner's 
initial or recommended decision to the agency's final decision. This 
interval exceeded one year in 15% of the cases reported. It was over 

6 months in 47% of the cases. 

Table 7 indicates the time consumed in the agency's decisional proc- 
ess, after public procedures are complete and nothing remains but to 
decide the case. It measures the interval from the date the matter 
was first ripe for agency decision (record certified by examiner, oral 
argument completed, last brief filed, etc.) to date of issuance of the 
agency's final decision. 

Of the 3,324 proceedings for which these data are available, agencies 
had under advisement for more than one year 287 cases, or approxi- 
mately 8.6% of the total. Over 28% were under advisement more 
than 6 months. 16.4% were decided in a month or less. 

13 



Part C — Hearing Examiner Volume and Backlog Data 

Parts C, D, and E repeat basic information contained not only in 
the 1958 compilation, but also in the 1957 compilation. They thereby 
afford comparison of the three years of experience under most items. 

Parts C and D concern only those proceedings reported in Parts A 
and B which at any time during the year were in the hands of hear- 
ing examiners for hearing and decision. If a proceeding, prior to 
July 1, 1958, had reached the stage where the examiner had issued 
his decision and thus disposed of the matter insofar as he was con- 
cerned, it is not reported in the 1959 figures in these tables even though 
it remained on the agency's docket for agency review after that date. 
Similarly, if a proceeding commenced prior to the end of the year 
had not been assigned to the agency's office of hearing examiners by 
June 30, it would not be reported in Parts C and D, although it was 
reported in Part A as pending before the agency during the year. 

Further, Parts C and D concern only so much of these proceedings 
as was in the hands of the office of hearing examiners, from the date 
the agency turned over the matter to the office or to the chief ex- 
aminer for hearing and decision, to the time when its pendency before 
an examiner terminates, whether or not that termination ultimately 
disposes of the proceeding as far as the agency is concerned. 

Table 8 gives the number of proceedings of each kind for each agency 
pending before examiners on July 1 of the years 1956, 1957, and 1958, 
input during each fiscal year in terms of new cases assigned to the 
office of hearing examiners and cases remanded or reassigned, exam- 
iners' output during each year, however these cases may have been 
disposed of by examiners, and the balance pending at the end of each 
year. 

The backlog of examiner cases pending at the end of the year was 
19,268 on June 30, 1958, and 18,150 on June 30, 1959. However, 
the decrease in Social Security cases was 1,912 in that period. During 
the 3-year period of these statistics the number of Social Security cases 
has increased greatly due to the extension of disability insurance bene- 
fits under the 1956 amendments to Title 11 of the Social Security Act. 
The Department of Health, Education, and Welfare hired 117 new 
hearing examiners between July 1, 1956 and July 1, 1959 to handle 
this tremendous volume of cases. Eliminating Social Security cases 
from the grand total figures, Table 8 shows that the number of pro- 
ceedings pending before examiners at the end of the year increased 
more than 16% from 1958 to 1959. 

Table 9 demonstrates the length of time that proceedings in the hands 
of examiners at the end of each year had been pending before examiners. 
The corresponding table in last year's compilation measured the period 
of time from the date of assignment of each proceeding to a particular 

14 



examiner. Because this is a difficult date to ascertain in most cases, 
Table 9 of this compilation measures the period from the date the 
agency assigned the proceeding to its office of hearing examiners or 
chief examiner, for hearing and decision. In most cases assignment to 
a particular examiner follows within a few days. The practice of the 
National Labor Relations Board is an exception. In most NLRB cases, 
because assignment depends upon examiners' itineraries and schedules, 
assignment to a particular examiner is withheld until a few days before 
the scheduled hearing. Prior to such assignment the chief examiner, or 
an examiner he assigns temporarily, rules upon motions and requests 
and otherwise controls the conduct of the case. 

It is felt that to the extent there is an interval between assignment 
to the office of hearing examiners and assignment to a particular ex- 
aminer, the period thereof should be included in the time intervals 
reported in Tables 9, 11, and 12. 

The grand total figures at the end of Table 9 indicate that the num- 
ber of proceedings which, at the end of the fiscal year 1959, had been 
pending before the office of hearing examiners more than one year was 
12.5% of the total pending. The number then pending before examiners 
3 months or less was 1,074, or 28% of the total. 

Table 10 demonstrates the manner in which pendency of proceedings 
before hearing examiners was terminated during fiscal years 1957, 1958, 
and 1959. Of the grand total of 75,280 proceedings disposed of by ex- 
aminers during the three-year period, 55,115 were disposed of by 
examiner decisions on the merits. 

Part D — Time Involved in Examiners' Handling of Cases 

Part D reports time-study data for proceedings disposed of by exam- 
iners by decisions on the merits after evidentiary hearings. These data 
are not available for Social Security, Indian probate, and Interstate 
Commerce Commission cases, except as indicated in the tables. 

Table 11 shows the over-all time that such proceedings were in the 
hands of examiners, from date of assignment of a proceeding to the 
office of hearing examiners to decision on the merits by an examiner. 
Corresponding tables in the 1957 and 1958 compilations measured this 
time interval from date of assignment to a particular examiner, rather 
than from date of assignment to the office of hearing examiners. 

In 1959, 7.1% of the proceedings disposed of by examiners by de- 
cisions on the merits had been in the hands of examiners more than 
one year, including whatever time there may have been between as- 
signment to the office of hearing examiners and assignment to a 
particular examiner. 

Table 12 is a new table in the 1959 compilation to show the length 
of time which was involved from assignment of a proceeding to the 
office of hearing examiners to completion of the evidentiary hearing. 

15 



As in Table 5, a hearing is considered completed for purposes of this 
table on the last day on which testimony or other evidence was placed 
in the record. 

The table indicates that in those proceedings for which this informa- 
tion is available, hearing was completed within 3 months after assign- 
ment to the office of hearing examiners in 72% of the cases. 

Table 13 replaces Table 11 in the 1958 compilation, entitled "Time 
Under Advisement by Examiners." That measurement is abandoned 
in this year's compilation because of the difficulty of ascertaining on 
what date a matter became ripe for examiner decision (last proposed 
findings, conclusions, and brief filed). 

Table 13 in the 1959 compilation indicates the interval from com- 
pletion of the evidentiary hearing to issuance of the examiner's decision. 

Table 14 repeats basic information appearing in the 1957 and 1958 
compilations concerning the examiner corps, adding corresponding in- 
formation for the fiscal year 1959. 

Under the first column heading, the figures show the number of 
examiners employed on July 1 of each of the years 1956-1959. Of 
the total 414 examiners employed on July 1, 1959, 167 were grade 
GS-15, 71 were grade GS-14, 167 were grade GS-13, and 9 were 
grade OS- 12. 

The figures representing man-years of examiner time available to 
each agency during each year take into account separations from and 
entrances into service as well as inter-agency loans of examiners. 

For purposes of the column which shows the average number of days 
per man-year spent by examiners presiding over hearings and pre- 
hearing or hearing conferences, the agencies were asked to report as a 
day of hearing any day on which the examiner presided over a hear- 
ing or conference, whether for the full day or only part of the day, 
and whether in one proceeding or more than one. The total number 
of days thus reported was divided by the agency's man-years figure in 
the preceding column to provide the figures shown in the column show- 
ing average number of days in hearing. 

Similarly, figures in the column showing average number of decisions 
per man-year were computed by dividing, for each agency, the total 
number of examiner decisions on the merits after hearing, as shown in 
the second column of Table 10, by the man-years figure in the second 
column of this table. 

The last column of Table 14 indicates the number of cases assigned to 
an examiner with the intention that he not issue a decision, but instead 
merely conduct the hearing and certify the record to the agency for its 
decision. This column does not include cases wherein the examiner 
would have issued a decision but for waiver thereof None of the cases 
in this column are reported elsewhere in this compilation, except that 
the time spent in hearings in these cases is reported, with the time spent 



16 



in hearings in all other cases, under the third column headings of 
Table 14. 

Data Contained in Previous Compilations 

Several of the tables included in the previous compilations which have 
been replaced by other data in this report are of continuing usefulness in 
studying agency procedures. For convenient reference to that informa- 
tion, it is listed below with indication as to where it may be found in the 
previous compilations. 

1. Use of ^'shortened procedures" which permit determination on 
the merits on the basis of written submissions without oral 
hearings (1957 Table IV). 

2. Comparison of time involved in disposition of "shortened pro- 
cedure" cases with time involved in regular hearing cases 
(1957 Table V). 

3. Use of prehearing conferences (1957 Table VIII). 

4. Time interval from -commencement of proceeding to assign- 
ment to a particular examiner (1958 Table 5). 

5. Time interval from assignment to an examiner to opening of 
the hearing (1957 Table VII). 

6. Length of hearings (1957 Table VIII). 

7. Time interval from close of hearing to final submission to ex- 
aminer (last brief and proposed findings and conclusions filed) 
(1957 Table VIII). 

8. Time under advisement by examiners (1958 Table 11). 

9. Comparison of number of withdrawals and dismissals before 
hearing with number after hearing (1957 Table II). 

10. Number of proceedings suspended because of interlocutory 
appeals (1957 Table VIII). 

1 1 . Number of proceedings in which hearings were reopened prior 
to examiner decision (1957 Table VIII). 

12. Temporary inter-agency transfers of hearing examiners (1957 
Table IX).

Footnotes to Table 1 



Although the AEC conducted a few proceedings in 1957 and 1958, using an examiner 
borrowed from the FCC, 1959 was the first year in which it had its own examiner 
staff and therefore the first year in which AEC figures are included in these 
compilations. 

On February 2, 1959 the AEC amended Part 3 of 10 CFR to substitute APA examiners 
for the Advisory Board on Contract Appeals in disputes articles appeal proceed- 
ings. (24 F.R. 726) 

Except for safety enforcement proceedings, all data on pending CAB cases include 
estimates as to the number of proceedings awaiting action and not yet in process. 
This is necessary because, although applications are committed to the hearing 
process at the time they are filed, the number of proceedings which will result 
is indeterminate because a number of applications are usually consolidated in 
each proceeding. For example, 221 route licensing proceedings were reported 
pending at the end of the year. The actual number of cases in process at the 
time was 41. The CAB estimated that an additional 180 proceedings would result 
from the 548 applications then awaiting action. 

In its figures for 1958, the CSC inadvertently included one proceeding which was 
not within the scope of these statistics. This case is omitted from the 1959 
figures. Four cases which had been considered abandoned and therefore were 
omitted from the 1958 figures were revived and are included in the 1959 figures, 

FCC data for this table for 1958 were not broken down by kinds of proceedings, but 
instead were consolidated so that only the totals are available. 

Obviously the balance pending at the end of 1958 should equal the number of pro- 
ceedings pending at the beginning of 1959. The reports of four agencies con- 
tained discrepancies in these figures, with the result that the grand total line 
shows a net discrepancy of 19 proceedings.

Footnotes to Table 3 



4^ 



1 The next to last column heading is intended to include all proceedings terminated 

by acquiescence or agreement, actual or presumed. Some of the 1958 proceedings 
reported in this column may have been dismissed for procedural defects or other- 
wise without the agreement of the parties. For example, 73 of the 88 Department 
of Agriculture cases reported in this column were consent order cases or consent 
dismissals. The remaining 15 were reported in the 1958 compilation simply as 
cases "dismissed or withdrawn without decision on the merits." 

2 Dismissed as moot. 

3 Dismissed for procedural deficiency or dismissed as moot. 

4 See Table 1, footnote 5o 

5 Consolidated with another proceeding. 

6 Proceedings instituted by the Commission, then withdrawn before hearing. 

7 All of these cases involved insurance companies. They were dismissed, after 

initial decision, for lack of jurisdiction on the basis of a test case, FTC v. 
National Casualty Co., 357 UoSo 560 (1958) „ 

8 Not timely filed. 

9 Dismissed for failure to file a Statement of Reasons in support of the appeal. 

10 Terminated upon motions to dismiss » 

11 Consolidated with other proceedings a 

12 One case indefinitely suspended pending decision in a related criminal case, 

1 placed in "appeal status" by stipulation, 1 determined by supplemental fraud 
order, 4 summarily determined by issuance of fraud orders upon violation of

Footnotes to Table 8 



Figures shown for 1957 and 1958 indicate the number of cases which had been assigned 
to individual hearing examiners for hearing and decision and were pending before 
them during the year, whereas the 1959 figures report all cases referred by the 
agency to the office of hearing examiners or to the chief examiner for hearing 
and decision, irrespective of whether they had, during the year, reached the stage 
where they were assigned to individual examiners. The difference probably is 
slight, since most cases are assigned to particular examiners immediately or 
within a few days after assignment to the office of hearing examiners. NLRB 
cases are an exception. Because assignment depends upon examiners* itineraries 
and schedules, assignment to a particular examiner is withheld until a few days 
before the scheduled hearing in most cases.

Footnotes to Table 10 



1 The word "agreement" in the fourth column heading includes acquiescence and presumed 

agreement as well as settlement or other actual agreement. The 1957 and 1958 
figures in this column may include some cases dismissed for procedural deficiency, 
untimeliness, etc. and without the consent of the parties. However, the number 
of cases thus misplaced is relatively small. Such cases in 1959 are appropriately 
reported in the last column entitled "Other examiner disposition." 

2 Dismissed as moot or dismissed for procedural deficiency. 

3 Decided on written submissions without oral hearings. 

4 One investigatory hearing cancelled. Five cases disposed of following such hearings. 

5 Dismissed.

Footnotes to Table 11 



1 1957 and 1958 figures demonstrate the interval from assignment to an individual 

examiner to decision by him. 1959 figures show the interval from assignment to the 
office of hearing examiners, rather than from assignment to a particular examiner. 

2 Time data not available. 

3 Totals exclude Indian probate matters, for which time data are not available. 

4 Rate case data for 1959 not available in the form requested. Average time was 9.3 months. 

5 Includes 12 reopened or remanded cases which are not included in the other columns 

because time data thereon are not available, 

6 Includes 3 cases not included in the other columns because data thereon not available, 
ro 7 Includes 4 cases not included in the other columns because data thereon not available, 

8 Includes 2 cases not included in the other columns because data thereon not available. 

9 Total includes 25 cases not reported in other columns. Time data not available. 

10 Information not available for 1957 fraud and "unlawful" order cases. 

11 The 1957 grand totals exclude 4,202 Social Security cases, 2,319 Indian probate cases, 

25 NLRB cases, and 41 Post Office Department cases for which data for this table are 
not available. The 1958 figures exclude Social Security and Indian probate cases. 
Grand totals for 1959 thus exclude Social Security cases, Indian probate cases, and 
149 Interstate Commerce Commission cases, for which these data are not available.

Footnotes to Table 13 



1 The time interval reflected by the 1959 figures in this table is not the same 

interval as that reported by the 1957 and 1958 figures. The 1957 and 1958 
data are taken from Table 11 of the 1958 compilation entitled "Time Under 
Advisement by Examiners." For purposes of that table, "time under advisement" 
was measured from the date of the last submission to the examiner, that is, 
in most cases, the date the last brief and proposed findings and conclusions 
were filed. The interval reported in the 1959 figures was measured from the 
date the hearing was completed. It thereby includes time required for the 
preparation and filing of briefs, 

2 Time data not available. 

3 Totals exclude Indian probate cases. 

4 Data for 1959 ICC cases are not available in the form requested. However, 
4^ averages were furnished as indicated. 

5 1957 NLRB information for this table is not available. 

6 The grand totals exclude Social Security and Indian probate cases. In addition, 

the 1957 figures exclude all NLRB cases, and the 1959 figures exclude all ICC 
cases. Data for this table ,are not available as to these proceedings.
